========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I came in because I can’t get my mind to stop jumping from one thing to the next. Like, at work in the lab, we have this new partnership, and I keep thinking, okay, what if that means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not useful enough? And then I’ll be looking at my data and suddenly I’m convinced maybe it’s wrong, or I missed something obvious, or I’m going to get blamed for it.

And it doesn’t really stay in one lane. It goes from work to money—like my bills piling up—to my family maybe thinking I’m falling behind compared to where I should be. And then I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow, even if I can’t point to one clear thing. It’s just… constant. I feel like my brain is always trying to find the next thing that might go wrong.

Not really, no. I mean, I try to. I’ll literally tell myself, like, “Just focus on the one thing in front of you. Finish the report. Check the assay. Read the numbers and move on.” But it doesn’t stay contained like that.

It’s like one worry catches on another one. I’ll be looking at the screen, trying to write something straightforward, and suddenly I’m not really reading anymore—I’m just sitting there while my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. Like if I make one mistake, then that means the whole thing is off, then someone notices, then it becomes this bigger problem. And even when part of me knows I’m spiraling, I can’t really shut it off. I can redirect for maybe a minute, but then it comes right back.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.

At work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.

And it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.

So, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.

I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.

At work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.

And I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I can’t get my mind to stop jumping from one thing to the next. Like, at work in the lab, we have this new partnership, and I keep thinking, okay, what if that means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not useful enough? And then I’ll be looking at my data and suddenly I’m convinced maybe it’s wrong, or I missed something obvious, or I’m going to get blamed for it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really stay in one lane. It goes from work to money—like my bills piling up—to my family maybe thinking I’m falling behind compared to where I should be. And then I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow, even if I can’t point to one clear thing. It’s just… constant. I feel like my brain is always trying to find the next thing that might go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there was a pretty clear shift, or at least that’s when it really started feeling like this. It’s been about seven months now. Around the time the lab’s funding changed and they brought in this commercial partnership, everything about how the place was being run started feeling different—less predictable, more like people were making decisions somewhere above me and I couldn’t tell what they meant for my job or for the work.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was one single day where it started, but that period is when my worrying really ramped up and just kind of… stayed. Since then it’s felt like I haven’t been able to get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to. I’ll literally tell myself, like, “Just focus on the one thing in front of you. Finish the report. Check the assay. Read the numbers and move on.” But it doesn’t stay contained like that.\n\nIt’s like one worry catches on another one. I’ll be looking at the screen, trying to write something straightforward, and suddenly I’m not really reading anymore—I’m just sitting there while my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. Like if I make one mistake, then that means the whole thing is off, then someone notices, then it becomes this bigger problem. And even when part of me knows I’m spiraling, I can’t really shut it off. I can redirect for maybe a minute, but then it comes right back.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at the lab were resolved, do you think your worrying would mostly go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. I mean, the lab situation is definitely a big part of it, and it’s kind of the thing my mind grabs onto first a lot of the time—like whether this new partnership means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not needed, or whether some mistake in my data is going to turn into this huge issue.\n\nBut it’s not only that anymore. Even if someone came in tomorrow and said, “Your job is completely safe and the lab is fine,” I feel like my brain would just… move to the next thing. It would go to my bills, or whether I’m falling behind compared to what my family expects, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow. It’s like the topic changes, but the worrying itself doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, definitely. I mean, the lab stuff is kind of the loudest worry right now, because it’s right in front of me every day, but it’s not contained there at all.

That’s actually why I came in, because my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m thinking about whether this new partnership at the lab means they’re going to cut my position, and then it turns into, “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” But then even when I’m not at work, it keeps going. I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.

It’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. I can’t just tell myself, “Okay, that’s a work problem, leave it at work,” because my brain just finds another thing to latch onto.

Like, at work in the lab, we have this new partnership, and I keep thinking, okay, what if that means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not useful enough? And then I’ll be looking at my data and suddenly I’m convinced maybe it’s wrong, or I missed something obvious, or I’m going to get blamed for it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really stay in one lane. It goes from work to money—like my bills piling up—to my family maybe thinking I’m falling behind compared to where I should be. And then I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow, even if I can’t point to one clear thing. It’s just… constant. I feel like my brain is always trying to find the next thing that might go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there was a pretty clear shift, or at least that’s when it really started feeling like this. It’s been about seven months now. Around the time the lab’s funding changed and they brought in this commercial partnership, everything about how the place was being run started feeling different—less predictable, more like people were making decisions somewhere above me and I couldn’t tell what they meant for my job or for the work.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was one single day where it started, but that period is when my worrying really ramped up and just kind of… stayed. Since then it’s felt like I haven’t been able to get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to. I’ll literally tell myself, like, “Just focus on the one thing in front of you. Finish the report. Check the assay. Read the numbers and move on.” But it doesn’t stay contained like that.\n\nIt’s like one worry catches on another one. I’ll be looking at the screen, trying to write something straightforward, and suddenly I’m not really reading anymore—I’m just sitting there while my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. Like if I make one mistake, then that means the whole thing is off, then someone notices, then it becomes this bigger problem. And even when part of me knows I’m spiraling, I can’t really shut it off. I can redirect for maybe a minute, but then it comes right back.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at the lab were resolved, do you think your worrying would mostly go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. I mean, the lab situation is definitely a big part of it, and it’s kind of the thing my mind grabs onto first a lot of the time—like whether this new partnership means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not needed, or whether some mistake in my data is going to turn into this huge issue.\n\nBut it’s not only that anymore. Even if someone came in tomorrow and said, “Your job is completely safe and the lab is fine,” I feel like my brain would just… move to the next thing. It would go to my bills, or whether I’m falling behind compared to what my family expects, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow. It’s like the topic changes, but the worrying itself doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the lab changes, have these worries also been happening in areas of your life that are unrelated to that stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the lab stuff is kind of the loudest worry right now, because it’s right in front of me every day, but it’s not contained there at all.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m thinking about whether this new partnership at the lab means they’re going to cut my position, and then it turns into, “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” But then even when I’m not at work, it keeps going. I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. Yeah… honestly, I think it is stronger than what’s actually going on, even though it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.

Like, there are real things happening — the lab partnership, the funding stuff, all of that — so I keep using that as proof that I’m being rational. I came in because it’s not just, “Oh, work is uncertain.” It’s like my brain immediately jumps from, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” to “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” and then to bills piling up, and then to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then to whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.

It’s like I can’t keep the worry proportional. One concern turns into five other concerns, and they all feel urgent, even if nothing has actually happened yet. So yeah, I think if I step back, the amount of worry is probably much bigger than what the situation calls for.

I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. Yeah, a lot. It’s actually one of the things I notice physically the most.

My shoulders and neck are just… tight almost all day, like they’re stuck up near my ears without me realizing it. I’ll be sitting at my computer going through spreadsheets or checking data, and then I suddenly notice I’m clenching my jaw really hard, like my teeth are pressed together for no reason. I mean, the lab situation is definitely a big part of it, and it’s kind of the thing my mind grabs onto first a lot of the time—like whether this new partnership means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not needed, or whether some mistake in my data is going to turn into this huge issue.\n\nBut it’s not only that anymore. Even if someone came in tomorrow and said, “Your job is completely safe and the lab is fine,” I feel like my brain would just… move to the next thing. It would go to my bills, or whether I’m falling behind compared to what my family expects, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow. It’s like the topic changes, but the worrying itself doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the lab changes, have these worries also been happening in areas of your life that are unrelated to that stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the lab stuff is kind of the loudest worry right now, because it’s right in front of me every day, but it’s not contained there at all.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m thinking about whether this new partnership at the lab means they’re going to cut my position, and then it turns into, “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” But then even when I’m not at work, it keeps going. I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. I can’t just tell myself, “Okay, that’s a work problem, leave it at work,” because my brain just finds another thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have these constant worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say yes. It’s been about seven months now, if I’m counting from when things changed at the lab — when the funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just a temporary reaction, like, “Okay, things are unstable, anyone would be stressed.” But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been there most days since then, just kind of following me around. Some days it’s louder than others, but I don’t think there have been many days where I felt genuinely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is stronger than would be expected for what’s actually happening in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I think it is stronger than what’s actually going on, even though it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nLike, there are real things happening — the lab partnership, the funding stuff, all of that — so I keep using that as proof that I’m being rational. But then my mind just takes it way beyond the actual facts. I came in because it’s not just, “Oh, work is uncertain.” It’s like my brain immediately jumps from, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” to “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” and then to bills piling up, and then to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then to whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nIt’s like I can’t keep the worry proportional. One concern turns into five other concerns, and they all feel urgent, even if nothing has actually happened yet. So yeah, I think if I step back, the amount of worry is probably much bigger than what the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s actually one of the things I notice physically the most.\n\nMy shoulders and neck are just… tight almost all day, like they’re stuck up near my ears without me realizing it. I’ll be sitting at my computer going through spreadsheets or checking data, and then I suddenly notice I’m clenching my jaw really hard, like my teeth are pressed together for no reason. I don’t even catch myself doing it until my face or neck starts to feel sore.\n\nBy the evening it feels like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly just been sitting. My neck gets stiff, my shoulders ache, and I’ll get these headaches that feel like they’re coming from the tension. It’s not just “I’m a little stressed” tightness — it feels like my body has been bracing for something all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not mainly, no… I mean, the lab changes are kind of where it starts a lot of the time, because that’s the obvious thing in front of me. Like I’ll think, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” and then I can’t just leave it there.

It sort of spreads out from that. Then I’m worrying whether my data are wrong, like maybe I missed something basic and everyone’s going to realize it. Then I start thinking about money and bills piling up if things go badly. Then it turns into whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.

So the lab is a big trigger, definitely, but it doesn’t stay contained there. My mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next until it feels like my whole life is on the line.

It’s been about seven months now. Since then it’s felt like I haven’t been able to get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to. I’ll be looking at the screen, trying to write something straightforward, and suddenly I’m not really reading anymore—I’m just sitting there while my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. Like if I make one mistake, then that means the whole thing is off, then someone notices, then it becomes this bigger problem. And even when part of me knows I’m spiraling, I can’t really shut it off. I can redirect for maybe a minute, but then it comes right back.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at the lab were resolved, do you think your worrying would mostly go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. I mean, the lab situation is definitely a big part of it, and it’s kind of the thing my mind grabs onto first a lot of the time—like whether this new partnership means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not needed, or whether some mistake in my data is going to turn into this huge issue.\n\nBut it’s not only that anymore. Even if someone came in tomorrow and said, “Your job is completely safe and the lab is fine,” I feel like my brain would just… move to the next thing. It would go to my bills, or whether I’m falling behind compared to what my family expects, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow. It’s like the topic changes, but the worrying itself doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the lab changes, have these worries also been happening in areas of your life that are unrelated to that stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the lab stuff is kind of the loudest worry right now, because it’s right in front of me every day, but it’s not contained there at all.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because my mind is constantly jumping around. I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. I can’t just tell myself, “Okay, that’s a work problem, leave it at work,” because my brain just finds another thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have these constant worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say yes. It’s been about seven months now, if I’m counting from when things changed at the lab — when the funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just a temporary reaction, like, “Okay, things are unstable, anyone would be stressed.” But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been there most days since then, just kind of following me around. Some days it’s louder than others, but I don’t think there have been many days where I felt genuinely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is stronger than would be expected for what’s actually happening in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I think it is stronger than what’s actually going on, even though it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nLike, there are real things happening — the lab partnership, the funding stuff, all of that — so I keep using that as proof that I’m being rational. One concern turns into five other concerns, and they all feel urgent, even if nothing has actually happened yet. So yeah, I think if I step back, the amount of worry is probably much bigger than what the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. I don’t even catch myself doing it until my face or neck starts to feel sore.\n\nBy the evening it feels like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly just been sitting. Then it turns into whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nSo the lab is a big trigger, definitely, but it doesn’t stay contained there. My mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next until it feels like my whole life is on the line.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore the changes at the lab, were you already someone who worried a lot about different parts of life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",

But then my mind just takes it way beyond the actual facts. I came in because it’s not just, “Oh, work is uncertain.” It’s like my brain immediately jumps from, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” to “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” and then to bills piling up, and then to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then to whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nIt’s like I can’t keep the worry proportional. One concern turns into five other concerns, and they all feel urgent, even if nothing has actually happened yet. So yeah, I think if I step back, the amount of worry is probably much bigger than what the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s actually one of the things I notice physically the most.\n\nMy shoulders and neck are just… tight almost all day, like they’re stuck up near my ears without me realizing it. I’ll be sitting at my computer going through spreadsheets or checking data, and then I suddenly notice I’m clenching my jaw really hard, like my teeth are pressed together for no reason. I don’t even catch myself doing it until my face or neck starts to feel sore.\n\nBy the evening it feels like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly just been sitting. My neck gets stiff, my shoulders ache, and I’ll get these headaches that feel like they’re coming from the tension. It’s not just “I’m a little stressed” tightness — it feels like my body has been bracing for something all day.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly centered on the changes at the lab?\n\nPatient: Not mainly, no… I mean, the lab changes are kind of where it starts a lot of the time, because that’s the obvious thing in front of me. Like I’ll think, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” and then I can’t just leave it there.\n\nIt sort of spreads out from that. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through maybe more than some people, but this level of worrying didn’t really start until about seven months ago, when the funding shift happened and the commercial partnership changed how everything at the lab was being run.

That was sort of the turning point. Before that, I could get stressed about school or work or family stuff, sure, but it didn’t feel like my mind was constantly branching out into every possible bad outcome. Since the lab changes, it’s like the uncertainty there set something off, and now it spreads into all these other areas instead of staying just about work. So… yeah, about seven months, and it’s been pretty steady since then.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I guess I came in because I can’t get my mind to stop jumping from one thing to the next. Like, at work in the lab, we have this new partnership, and I keep thinking, okay, what if that means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not useful enough? And then I’ll be looking at my data and suddenly I’m convinced maybe it’s wrong, or I missed something obvious, or I’m going to get blamed for it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really stay in one lane. It goes from work to money—like my bills piling up—to my family maybe thinking I’m falling behind compared to where I should be. And then I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow, even if I can’t point to one clear thing. It’s just… constant. I feel like my brain is always trying to find the next thing that might go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there was a pretty clear shift, or at least that’s when it really started feeling like this. It’s been about seven months now. Around the time the lab’s funding changed and they brought in this commercial partnership, everything about how the place was being run started feeling different—less predictable, more like people were making decisions somewhere above me and I couldn’t tell what they meant for my job or for the work.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was one single day where it started, but that period is when my worrying really ramped up and just kind of… stayed. Since then it’s felt like I haven’t been able to get back to normal.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, do you feel able to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to. I’ll literally tell myself, like, “Just focus on the one thing in front of you. Finish the report. Check the assay. Read the numbers and move on.” But it doesn’t stay contained like that.\n\nIt’s like one worry catches on another one. I’ll be looking at the screen, trying to write something straightforward, and suddenly I’m not really reading anymore—I’m just sitting there while my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. Like if I make one mistake, then that means the whole thing is off, then someone notices, then it becomes this bigger problem. And even when part of me knows I’m spiraling, I can’t really shut it off. I can redirect for maybe a minute, but then it comes right back.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at the lab were resolved, do you think your worrying would mostly go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. I mean, the lab situation is definitely a big part of it, and it’s kind of the thing my mind grabs onto first a lot of the time—like whether this new partnership means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not needed, or whether some mistake in my data is going to turn into this huge issue.\n\nBut it’s not only that anymore. Even if someone came in tomorrow and said, “Your job is completely safe and the lab is fine,” I feel like my brain would just… move to the next thing. It would go to my bills, or whether I’m falling behind compared to what my family expects, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow. It’s like the topic changes, but the worrying itself doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the lab changes, have these worries also been happening in areas of your life that are unrelated to that stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the lab stuff is kind of the loudest worry right now, because it’s right in front of me every day, but it’s not contained there at all.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m thinking about whether this new partnership at the lab means they’re going to cut my position, and then it turns into, “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” But then even when I’m not at work, it keeps going. I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. I can’t just tell myself, “Okay, that’s a work problem, leave it at work,” because my brain just finds another thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have these constant worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say yes. It’s been about seven months now, if I’m counting from when things changed at the lab — when the funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just a temporary reaction, like, “Okay, things are unstable, anyone would be stressed.” But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been there most days since then, just kind of following me around. Some days it’s louder than others, but I don’t think there have been many days where I felt genuinely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is stronger than would be expected for what’s actually happening in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I think it is stronger than what’s actually going on, even though it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nLike, there are real things happening — the lab partnership, the funding stuff, all of that — so I keep using that as proof that I’m being rational. But then my mind just takes it way beyond the actual facts. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. I’ll literally tell myself, like, “Just focus on the one thing in front of you. Finish the report. Check the assay. Read the numbers and move on.” But it doesn’t stay contained like that.\n\nIt’s like one worry catches on another one. I’ll be looking at the screen, trying to write something straightforward, and suddenly I’m not really reading anymore—I’m just sitting there while my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. Like if I make one mistake, then that means the whole thing is off, then someone notices, then it becomes this bigger problem. And even when part of me knows I’m spiraling, I can’t really shut it off. I can redirect for maybe a minute, but then it comes right back.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at the lab were resolved, do you think your worrying would mostly go back to normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. I mean, the lab situation is definitely a big part of it, and it’s kind of the thing my mind grabs onto first a lot of the time—like whether this new partnership means they’re going to cut my position, or decide I’m not needed, or whether some mistake in my data is going to turn into this huge issue.\n\nBut it’s not only that anymore. Even if someone came in tomorrow and said, “Your job is completely safe and the lab is fine,” I feel like my brain would just… move to the next thing. It would go to my bills, or whether I’m falling behind compared to what my family expects, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow. It’s like the topic changes, but the worrying itself doesn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your work or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It has, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work it’s been the most obvious. I actually missed an internal lab deadline recently, and that’s not really like me. It wasn’t because I didn’t care or because I didn’t have the information — I had the data, I had the pieces — but I just could not organize myself enough to get the writing and the data review done in a clean way. I kept going back over things, second-guessing what I’d written, checking numbers again, then realizing I’d spent all this time and still hadn’t actually finished the report. It felt like my brain was busy the whole time but I wasn’t producing anything useful.\n\nAnd it’s started spilling into normal life too. Stuff that used to feel manageable now feels like this huge list I’m already behind on. Preparing routine reports feels overwhelming. Showing up to art nights — which used to be something I looked forward to — now feels like another thing I might fail at or not have energy for. Even cooking for my family, which shouldn’t be that complicated, can feel like too much to keep up with. I’ll think about what needs to be done and it’s like everything piles up at once, so I either put it off or I do it while feeling completely tense the whole time.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely interfering. It’s not just thoughts in the background anymore. It’s affecting what I actually get done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge a lot of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s not even just mentally on edge, it’s like my body is wound up too.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit down at my desk and I just can’t really settle into the chair. I’ll be trying to focus, but my leg is bouncing under the bench, or I’m tapping my foot, or shifting around like I’m uncomfortable even if nothing is actually wrong with the chair or the room. I catch myself checking messages over and over, not because I’m expecting anything specific, but because I feel like I need to do something.\n\nAnd I keep getting up for no real reason. Like I’ll walk back to the instrument room even though I was just there, or I’ll go check something that doesn’t actually need checking, just because sitting still feels impossible. Even normal lab sounds — someone shutting a drawer, a machine clicking, a cart moving — make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s not like anything dangerous happened. I’m just already so keyed up that everything feels like it hits me too hard.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the lab changes, have these worries also been happening in areas of your life that are unrelated to that stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the lab stuff is kind of the loudest worry right now, because it’s right in front of me every day, but it’s not contained there at all.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m thinking about whether this new partnership at the lab means they’re going to cut my position, and then it turns into, “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” But then even when I’m not at work, it keeps going. I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. I can’t just tell myself, “Okay, that’s a work problem, leave it at work,” because my brain just finds another thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have these constant worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say yes. It’s been about seven months now, if I’m counting from when things changed at the lab — when the funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just a temporary reaction, like, “Okay, things are unstable, anyone would be stressed.” But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been there most days since then, just kind of following me around. Some days it’s louder than others, but I don’t think there have been many days where I felt genuinely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is stronger than would be expected for what’s actually happening in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I think it is stronger than what’s actually going on, even though it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nLike, there are real things happening — the lab partnership, the funding stuff, all of that — so I keep using that as proof that I’m being rational. But then my mind just takes it way beyond the actual facts. I came in because it’s not just, “Oh, work is uncertain.” It’s like my brain immediately jumps from, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” to “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” and then to bills piling up, and then to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then to whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nIt’s like I can’t keep the worry proportional. One concern turns into five other concerns, and they all feel urgent, even if nothing has actually happened yet. So yeah, I think if I step back, the amount of worry is probably much bigger than what the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s actually one of the things I notice physically the most.\n\nMy shoulders and neck are just… tight almost all day, like they’re stuck up near my ears without me realizing it. I’ll be sitting at my computer going through spreadsheets or checking data, and then I suddenly notice I’m clenching my jaw really hard, like my teeth are pressed together for no reason. I don’t even catch myself doing it until my face or neck starts to feel sore.\n\nBy the evening it feels like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly just been sitting. My neck gets stiff, my shoulders ache, and I’ll get these headaches that feel like they’re coming from the tension. It’s not just “I’m a little stressed” tightness — it feels like my body has been bracing for something all day.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly centered on the changes at the lab?\n\nPatient: Not mainly, no… I mean, the lab changes are kind of where it starts a lot of the time, because that’s the obvious thing in front of me. Like I’ll think, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” and then I can’t just leave it there.\n\nIt sort of spreads out from that. Then I’m worrying whether my data are wrong, like maybe I missed something basic and everyone’s going to realize it. Then I start thinking about money and bills piling up if things go badly. Then it turns into whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nSo the lab is a big trigger, definitely, but it doesn’t stay contained there. My mind keeps jumping from one thing to the next until it feels like my whole life is on the line.\n\nDoctor: Before the changes at the lab, were you already someone who worried a lot about different parts of life?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through maybe more than some people, but this level of worrying didn’t really start until about seven months ago, when the funding shift happened and the commercial partnership changed how everything at the lab was being run.\n\nThat was sort of the turning point. Before that, I could get stressed about school or work or family stuff, sure, but it didn’t feel like my mind was constantly branching out into every possible bad outcome. Since the lab changes, it’s like the uncertainty there set something off, and now it spreads into all these other areas instead of staying just about work. So… yeah, about seven months, and it’s been pretty steady since then.\n\nDoctor: Has the worrying continued for more than six months since the changes at the lab began?\n\nPatient: Yes — it’s been more than six months. I’d say about seven months now, starting around when the lab’s funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a temporary reaction, like, okay, things are unstable at work and I’m just stressed because nobody really knows what’s happening. But it didn’t settle down after a few weeks. It just kept going, and now looking back, it’s basically been steady since then. So yeah… about seven months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I honestly don’t know. I’ve thought about that a lot, actually, because part of me wants to say, “No, if the lab hadn’t changed, I’d probably be fine,” because that really did feel like the thing that kicked all of this off.

I’ll start thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like the subject changes, but the feeling is the same. I can’t just tell myself, “Okay, that’s a work problem, leave it at work,” because my brain just finds another thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Have these constant worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say yes. It’s been about seven months now, if I’m counting from when things changed at the lab — when the funding shifted and that commercial partnership started changing how everything was being run.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just a temporary reaction, like, “Okay, things are unstable, anyone would be stressed.” But it didn’t really settle down. It’s been there most days since then, just kind of following me around. Some days it’s louder than others, but I don’t think there have been many days where I felt genuinely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your level of worry is stronger than would be expected for what’s actually happening in your life right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, I think it is stronger than what’s actually going on, even though it doesn’t feel that way in the moment.\n\nLike, there are real things happening — the lab partnership, the funding stuff, all of that — so I keep using that as proof that I’m being rational. But then my mind just takes it way beyond the actual facts. I came in because it’s not just, “Oh, work is uncertain.” It’s like my brain immediately jumps from, “What if this new partnership means they cut my position?” to “What if my data are wrong and I missed something obvious?” and then to bills piling up, and then to whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and then to whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nIt’s like I can’t keep the worry proportional. One concern turns into five other concerns, and they all feel urgent, even if nothing has actually happened yet. So yeah, I think if I step back, the amount of worry is probably much bigger than what the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. It’s actually one of the things I notice physically the most.\n\nMy shoulders and neck are just… tight almost all day, like they’re stuck up near my ears without me realizing it. I’ll be sitting at my computer going through spreadsheets or checking data, and then I suddenly notice I’m clenching my jaw really hard, like my teeth are pressed together for no reason. I don’t even catch myself doing it until my face or neck starts to feel sore.\n\nBy the evening it feels like I’ve been hauling equipment around all day, even if I’ve mostly just been sitting. My neck gets stiff, my shoulders ache, and I’ll get these headaches that feel like they’re coming from the tension. It’s not just “I’m a little stressed” tightness — it feels like my body has been bracing for something all day.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly centered on the changes at the lab?\n\nPatient: Not mainly, no… I mean, the lab changes are kind of where it starts a lot of the time, because that’s the obvious thing in front of me. It’s hard to separate that out, because I can’t really rewind and test it.\n\nI think without the lab uncertainty, I probably wouldn’t be at this level. Or at least I don’t think it would have gotten this intense. But I’m not completely sure, because now that it’s happening, it feels like it’s attached to everything, not just the lab. So… I guess my answer is I don’t know for sure, but I suspect the lab situation was the main thing that set it off.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel your anxiety is mainly a reaction to the changes at the lab?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / chemist_or_materials_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from whether our lab's new partnership is going to cut my position to whether my data are wrong, my bills are going to pile up, my family thinks I'm falling behind, and whether I've already ruined my chances at grad school.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it started as a reaction to the lab changes, yeah. That’s still the center of it in a lot of ways — like whether this new partnership means my position gets cut, or whether the priorities are going to shift and I won’t have a place there anymore.

But it doesn’t stay neatly contained there. That’s the problem. My mind jumps from that to, okay, what if my data are wrong, what if I’ve missed something obvious, what if this affects my future in the field. Then suddenly I’m thinking about bills piling up, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, and whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.

So… yes, the lab changes feel like the thing that set it off, but now the anxiety is bigger than just that one situation. It’s like the lab uncertainty opened the door, and now everything feels like something I can worry myself into a corner about.
